that even at NeurIPS, the biggest gathering of AI researchers in the world, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fQz6afpcZhdMdYzgE/my-hobby-running-deranged-surveys"><span>a third of the AI researchers don’t know what AGI stands for</span></a><span>, but yeah, we are nowhere near.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnr6b7eqs9orh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefr6b7eqs9orh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I now think that loss of control is probably more urgent than biorisks. See </span><a href="https://www.pourdemain.ngo/en/post/loss-of-control-closing-the-governance-gap-before-the-first-incident"><span>this analysis</span></a><span> from PourDemain. I think loss of control is orders of magnitude more likely than irreversible x-risks from AI-enabled pandemics, even if both are probably already at intolerable levels.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0wkw3j3orsi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0wkw3j3orsi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We matched on exact strings.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn44cceo1mjeh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref44cceo1mjeh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>When people are asked about AI directly, they often express concern; but when they are asked what political issues matter most, AI risk is usually absent or marginal. Concretely: YouGov's "AI will negatively affect society" rose from 34% (Dec 2024) to 47% (June 2025), yet AI does not appear at all in Pew's 24-item ranking of top national problems (Feb 2025) and sits near 1% in Gallup's open-ended "most important problem."</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyu0801n397a"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyu0801n397a">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>And the comparison is cross-pollster rather than head-to-head (no single neutral poll ranks them against each other). Pew (Aug 2024) finds people "highly concerned" about misinformation (66%), loss of human connection (57%), job loss (56%) and bias (55%); a separate YouGov poll (June 2025) puts concern about AI-driven human extinction at 43%.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnoue5twoqry"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefoue5twoqry">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Did you know that the Mexican government has already been hacked by an unattributed hacker using Claude? (Bloomberg, Feb 2026) It seems that, in an authorized red-team test, a frontier model reportedly compromised most of the NSA's classified systems within hours (</span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/06/23/us/politics/nsa-lost-access-anthropic-tool.html?eafs_enabled=false"><span>NYT</span></a><span>). The fact that this is not the top story in the world is also revealing about the information ecosystem.</span></p><p><span>But again, this is not really a factor under our control, and I argue in this post that </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RYx6cLwzoajqjyB6b/what-convincing-warning-shot-could-help-prevent-extinction"><span>convincing warning shots are unlikely</span></a><span>. I come back to this in the sub-section “</span><i><span>The main objection: Let’s just wait for a warning shot? I don’t think this works.”</span></i></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngk9udkwfluj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgk9udkwfluj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>There are more lobbyists working on the EU's digital files (890 FTE in 2025) than there are MEPs (720). (Corporate Europe Observatory,</span><a href="https://corporateeurope.org/en/2025/10/big-tech-lobby-budgets-hit-record-levels"><span> Big Tech lobby budgets hit record levels</span></a><span>)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnj96dmcvak6c"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefj96dmcvak6c">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span> 202.5 vs 55.75 FTE, Green-Lowe / CAIP,</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/4PLuwEyMZhT89Dxpo"><span> </span><i><span>An Activist View of AI Governance</span></i></a><span>, 2025; author's estimate.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnge67dnk30n"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefge67dnk30n">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I don't know the grantmakers' specific reasons, and they may have been good ones. But I have the feeling that the ecosystem's revealed preference is that a new research org is easier to fund than an advocacy org.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw2ka9zevyco"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw2ka9zevyco">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In advertising, the exact threshold of repetition has been debated since Krugman's 'Why Three Exposures May Be Enough' (1972) — two, three, four? — but the general effect is well established.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpb0xi00qtgm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpb0xi00qtgm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>(or at least to reduce this parameter in their weighted factor models)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6jdw5y5a81q"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6jdw5y5a81q">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I cannot name them publicly, but DM if you want private proof.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fne9nft9iuc1o"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefe9nft9iuc1o">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The comparison is loose; UNFCCC observers also include industry groups and all kinds of NGOs, while I'm counting only non-technical AI safety organizations (which is also generous in some respects). Regardless, I think that even with heavy discounting, the gap is enormous.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnj7c0p0uv6c"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefj7c0p0uv6c">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Remember also that the environmental movement is over fifty years old. Rome wasn't built in a day.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5ahnsvfawfd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5ahnsvfawfd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gW3XMJGgrSJ5hKyjD/where-i-am-donating-in-2025"><span>Michael Dickens's 2025 donor review</span></a><span> also concludes advocacy is far more neglected than research and that the few advocacy orgs do not get much grantmaker support.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fni47tsto5jfl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefi47tsto5jfl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The insider game has documented integrity costs, and there is also a missing mood (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/vFqa8DZCuhyrbSnyx/integrity-in-ai-governance-and-advocacy"><span>Integrity in AI Governance and Advocacy</span></a><span>). But I also think that it can be done correctly. E.g., I don’t think the most effective strategy is necessarily to open with the full Doom argument, and there are many strategies that lead to effective results and indirectly lead to more political will.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjbdjvwaerv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjbdjvwaerv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Why are Neuralese bad? See this: </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11473"><span>Chain of Thought Monitorability: A New and Fragile Opportunity for AI Safety</span></a><span> </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvuojbjkoqd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvuojbjkoqd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I’m not sure Europe2031 was net positive. </span><a href="https://maxvonthun.substack.com/p/what-europe-2031-gets-wrong"><span>See</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnc9dsmoggz8d"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefc9dsmoggz8d">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Maybe a wild proposal, but one way to bypass institutional stigma is to create safe channels for civil servants, policymakers, and lab insiders to express what they already think privately. Maybe organizing anonymous joint statements?</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0y9y8qx02c6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0y9y8qx02c6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I must now admit that we should have pushed harder to explain the risks during the red lines campaign: removing the detailed explanation from the FAQ was a mistake.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3cvuvyaxl7a"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3cvuvyaxl7a">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The article "</span><a href="https://www.machinocene.com/p/diplomacy-on-a-deadline?hide_intro_popup=true" rel="noreferrer"><span>diplomacy on a deadline</span></a><span>" explains how, historically, we were able to negotiate treaties quickly. Spoiler: feeling a sense of urgency helps tremendously, among other things.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncyoszefzflm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcyoszefzflm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><i><span>“A lot of semi-invisible, ongoing-over-years, and hard-to-definitively-attribute work has gone into many of the policy 'successes' of recent years. And sometimes part of the difficulty in attribution is actually down to things like the social proofing of multiple groups providing independent evidence and testimony that adds up to a credible body of expert input on a topic.” –  </span></i><a href="https://x.com/CRSegerie/status/2073438216640737363"><i><span>source</span></i></a><i><span>, Seán Ó hÉigeartaigh</span></i></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnicn91y1b9rj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreficn91y1b9rj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It's been more than a year since the constitutional classifier paper was published, and there is still no ready-to-use library. This is a coordination failure because the same work is duplicated over and over, and because this is currently one of the most effective strategies for reducing misuse. Currently, a small team in each lab might be tasked with reimplementing the best mitigation strategies (which include CC), and safety teams at smaller labs are often just a handful of people. If this library were maintained and updated as new vulnerabilities emerge, my guess is that it would free up a lot of time for safety teams across different labs. This is high-leverage because it would raise the floor for the whole ecosystem.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnh6grk2dfjic"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefh6grk2dfjic">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See </span><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-crux-list"><span>Zvi's Crux List</span></a><span> for much more than you have ever asked for.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn88enf2mkc1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref88enf2mkc1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>More generally, I think that our community has a tendency to ignore the ecosystem and work in silos, disconnected from the institutions and international fora, while that’s where the governance discourse happens.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnm5xdbplx7d"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefm5xdbplx7d">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Yes, politics is the mind killer, but only because smart people disengage from it. This is a hyperstition that needs to stop.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EexsebbYhbe2gXkPP/the-current-bottleneck-is-political-will-not-research#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Plan A: Suggestions For Further Work](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/B4qMZnB4a5YgKiLNo/plan-a-suggestions-for-further-work)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Yesterday we released </span><a href="http://ai-2040.com/"><span>AI 2040: Plan A</span></a><span>, but there's lots of work left to do. </span></p><p><span>We still have tons of uncertainty about the future of AI and the best strategies for how humanity can successfully chart a path through the intelligence explosion. You can read much of our current thinking on </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements"><span>the supplements page</span></a><span>. </span></p><p><span>In this post, I'll outline some research areas related to the feasibility/desirability of Plan A that I am excited about further research into. </span></p><p><span>We at AI Futures Project will do some of this work ourselves, so if you’re making a serious attempt, please get in touch. (Or </span><a href="https://airtable.com/appmcCGBz3vqARP9n/pag2bpNfoazMbunLZ/form" rel="noreferrer"><span>apply to work with us directly</span></a><span>). </span></p><p><span>(Note that many of the below proposals are only relevant in something like a Plan A world where we’ve slowed the intelligence explosion massively). </span></p><h2><span>Write Additional Prescriptive Scenarios </span></h2><p><span>There are </span><a href="https://time.com/6266923/ai-eliezer-yudkowsky-open-letter-not-enough/"><span>many</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/561e7512-253e-424b-9734-ef4098440601/Industrial%20Policy%20for%20the%20Intelligence%20Age.pdf"><span>other</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://darioamodei.com/post/policy-on-the-ai-exponential"><span>proposals</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.whitehouse.gov/wp-content/uploads/2025/07/Americas-AI-Action-Plan.pdf"><span>for</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.nationalsecurity.ai/"><span>how</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://situational-awareness.ai/"><span>to</span></a><span> navigate the intelligence explosion. Some of these plans are promising, some less so. However, none of them have been publicly gamed out in scenario format in nearly as much depth as Plan A. Instead, they tend to operate at a more abstract level that attempts to be consistent with many possible futures. Unfortunately, this also makes it much more difficult to analyze how good each of these plans are, because the tradeoffs have not been explored in depth in any possible worlds, and because it’s more likely that there are hidden difficulties or contradictions in a plan if it hasn’t been gamed out concretely even once. </span></p><p><span>I would like to build out a large set of competing concrete scenarios, some predictive, and some prescriptive (i.e. laying out concrete plans). I'm especially excited about doing this for plans that are potentially competitive with Plan A, including: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Plan S:</span></b><span> </span><b><span>Indefinite halt. </span></b><span>A halt on all frontier AI capabilities progress, intended to last at least a few years. Different variants of Plan S have different conditions for resumption of AI progress; for example it could be alignment progress, lie detectors, human uploads, or intelligence enhancement.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Domestic-first Plan A. </span></b><span>Regulate AI domestically enough to reduce AI takeover risk to acceptable levels, which will require a long slowdown. It’s possible other countries will also regulate domestically and something like Plan A won’t be needed; otherwise, transition to Plan A later. This is similar to what Richard gestures at in </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/selective-optimism-a-critique-of"><span>his critique of AI 2040</span></a><span>. </span></li><li value="3"><b><span>GPU Arms Control. </span></b><span>International agreements for countries to limit their GPU stock or flow, analogous to historical arms reduction agreements.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>CERN for AI. An international project to develop frontier AI,</span></b><span> with all other projects regulated to be substantially behind in capabilities.</span></li></ul><p><span>See more discussion of these options </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/?choices=plan-a-root#other-plans-that-are-competitive-with-plan-a"><span>here</span></a><span> in our scenario. </span></p><p><span>A second class of prescriptive scenarios that would be useful are scenarios that are similar at a high level to Plan A, but with some significant perturbation(s). For example, </span><a href="https://newsletter.forethought.org/p/plan-as-problem-with-dry-tinder?utm_source=post-email-title&amp;publication_id=4116210&amp;post_id=206421357&amp;utm_campaign=email-post-title&amp;isFreemail=true&amp;r=fa0ub&amp;triedRedirect=true&amp;utm_medium=email"><span>Tom Davidson critiques</span></a><span> Plan A for adding “dry tinder”, i.e. lots of additional compute that is being prevented from doing capabilities research on the critical path for the intelligence explosion, but if the regulatory regime were to break down, could be quickly redirected. One could imagine a version of Plan A that addresses this concern by limiting capabilities primarily via limiting compute instead of algorithms. This has some other benefits as well like being more enforceable than our version of Plan A. </span></p><p><span>More generally, I'm excited about scenarios like Plan A that vary anything described in the </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/plan-a-assumptions" rel="noreferrer"><span>assumptions supplement</span></a><span>. </span></p><p><span>Finally, I'm also excited about scenarios for </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/comparing-possible-plans#plans-that-we-think-are-clearly-worse-than-plan-a"><span>plans that are less desirable</span></a><span> than Plan A which may be easier to implement. </span></p><h2><span>Covert Project Further Research </span></h2><p><span>We present our current best guess modeling of covert projects </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/covert-ai-projects"><span>here</span></a><span>. However, this is an area where many on our team had different intuitions during the writing process, and we continue to have substantial uncertainty. Overall, it seems very tractable to significantly improve the analysis with further effort, as well as to identify additional levers to prevent, deter, or detect covert projects.  </span></p><p><span>Some particularly important areas of further research: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Are there near term actions that governments or private actors could take that would dramatically reduce the viability of covert projects? </span></li><li value="2"><span>Will it be possible to tell early on whether weight theft can be prevented? In our scenario, we assume that the consortium quickly becomes confident in their respective mitigations and can resume scaling in January 2030. That timeline isn’t loadbearing: if they were </span><i><span>obviously not</span></i><span> </span><i><span>confident</span></i><span> in their mitigations, they could remain paused until they were. But it is somewhat loadbearing that consensus can eventually be achieved on whether weight theft is possible.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Come up with better ways to bound the capabilities gain from distillation. Our current mitigations are </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/verification-plan#inference-restrictions-monitoring-and-refusals-for-high-risk-domains"><span>here</span></a><span>. </span></li><li value="4"><span>How much do R&amp;D compute reductions slow takeoff? Our current best guess is that </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7jcPg79p3kD5ir3CL/how-much-slower-does-takeoff-go-with-10-less-compute"><span>10x less compute causes the intelligence explosion to go 6x slower</span></a><span> (80% CI: 3.5, 8). This modeling is central to effectiveness estimates of any compute governance interventions, which are the primary lever for slowing down AI capabilities progress. </span><ul><li value="1"><span>Most of our uncertainty here comes from our uncertainty over the current rate of </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/gradient-updates/the-least-understood-driver-of-ai-progress"><span>software progress</span></a><span>, so gaining clarity on that would help.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h2><span>US Domestic Governance</span></h2><p><span>In AI 2040, we briefly discussed some important aspects of US governance. By this I mean both narrow questions about how to structure AI regulation (relevant section </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/?choices=plan-a-root#example-detailed-regulatory-process"><span>here</span></a><span>) as well as broader questions about how government and society is structured after extremely advanced AI has been built and diffused into the world. This is a part of AI 2040 we are especially uncertain about because it’s an inherently social and political question, and it’s also further outside our domains of expertise. So, we’d be excited for more people to think about how US domestic governance would evolve over the course of a Plan A scenario and make improved proposals. </span></p><p><span>Some example questions to think about:  </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Make a proposal for how to structure an AI regulatory apparatus. Ideally, this regulator would have good epistemics, won’t get captured, has good internal incentives, and is incentive compatible for existing power structures to empower. Our proposal </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/?choices=plan-a-root#five-centuries-in-five-years-what-pausing-at-human-level-feels-like"><span>here</span></a><span>. </span></li><li value="2"><span>Make a proposal for measures to improve generic government functionality. Governments will be under extreme pressure during the intelligence explosion: there will likely be tens of trillions of dollars of economic pressure from industry to avoid regulation, but also immense anti-AI public pressure for regulation. However, it will also be a period of intense social change, and government improvement proposals that are impossible today may become viable. There are a wide range of proposals that I think might be good here ranging from relatively simple (e.g. reforms to make it easier to hire technical talent into the federal government) to extreme changes (e.g. constitutional amendments that massively reform the way the government is structured). </span></li></ul><h2><span>Verification </span></h2><p><span>We discuss our current modeling in the </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/verification-plan"><span>Verification Plan</span></a><span>, and have a </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/verification-plan/get-involved"><span>get involved</span></a><span> page. </span></p><p><span>Some areas I’m excited particularly excited about include: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Build inference-only retrofit capacity (see </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/?choices=plan-a-root#what-if-they-didnt-have-inference-only-verification-ready"><span>maintext</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/verification-plan#feb-2029-inference-only-retrofit-begins"><span>detailed version</span></a><span>). Aspects of this are </span><a href="https://amododesign.com/ai-verification/plan-a-sitrep/"><span>in progress</span></a><span>, but more work needs to be done. </span></li><li value="2"><span>Attempt to accelerate “AI Silver Bullet” technologies (See Section 3.1, Method #5 of the </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/covert-ai-projects"><span>covert project supplement</span></a><span>), in particular: (i) privacy preserving AI auditing and (ii) lie detectors. We discuss downsides of lie detectors </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/?choices=plan-a-root#should-lie-detectors-be-allowed-banned-or-regulated"><span>here</span></a><span>, but on net, I think that accelerating their development would be good, if it is done in an open / transparent way so that the technology can’t be captured by one actor or government </span></li></ul><h2><span>Economics </span></h2><p><span>You can read our supplement </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/economics-of-plan-a"><span>here</span></a><span> and our Plan A model </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/econ-explorer"><span>here</span></a><span>. </span></p><p><span>There are two very distinct schools of thought with respect to the economics of AI. The mainstream economist view is that the current 3% GDP growth per year trend will continue for the foreseeable future. My view is that we’ll get explosive economic growth driven by superhuman AIs, of the sort that we see in the AI 2040 scenario, except much more extreme if we fail to make appropriate regulations on the intelligence or industrial explosion. </span></p><p><span>Unfortunately, in large part because of this divide, very little economics work has been done that takes AGI seriously (though there are a few notable exceptions). Moreover, we have large uncertainty about many aspects of the economy before, during, and after the intelligence explosion. Better modeling would improve our understanding of a crucial aspect of the future, and thus enable us to choose better response strategies. This is especially important in worlds like Plan A; where governance leads to a more drawn out intelligence explosion than I expect, but it's also important in natural slow takeoff worlds. </span></p><p><span>Some concrete projects on the economics of AI that I’m excited about are: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Make a better version of </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/economics-of-plan-a#the-full-economic-model"><span>our economics model</span></a><span>.  We aren’t economists, and only had time to make a relatively scrappy model. I’m confident it’s possible to do a much better analysis with more time or expertise than we have. </span></li><li value="2"><span>Think about robot doubling times in realistic scenarios, e.g. each of plan A-D. The best analysis I’m aware of is </span><a href="https://defensesindepth.bio/ai-industrial-takeoff-part-1-maximum-growth-rates-with-current-technology/"><span>this series</span></a><span> by Damon Binder. I think a valuable next step would be to try to make a reasonable full fledged model of the industrial explosion, maybe on top of the </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/"><span>AI Futures Model</span></a><span>. </span></li><li value="3"><span>Build a model of what would happen to the AI industry in Plan A vs Plan S vs Plan D, which includes valuations, margins, number of companies at the frontier, etc.</span></li></ul><h2><span>Various Plan A Details </span></h2><p><span>Here’s a final list of other details which we didn’t have the chance to work out fully during the writing of Plan A, but which would be valuable projects that could strengthen Plan A: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>In AI 2040 we make a </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/?choices=plan-a-root#limiting-ai-persuasion-and-manipulation"><span>proposal</span></a><span> for limiting the persuasive capabilities of AI. However, we aren’t very concrete about the exact operationalization of the policy. To actually implement something like this, we’ll need a concrete set of standards for what is and isn’t allowed. This seems extremely important for preserving human autonomy in an AI age, but also involves some philosophically thorny questions: what exactly is the difference between “helping someone learn new things” and “convincing them that you are right”. </span></li><li value="2"><span>Write up the ambitious D/acc plan. During Plan A, there is a massive budget for defense. What’s the best way to spend that budget? Work out the $100T defense plan for the mid 2030s in Plan A for bio/cyber/persuasion and other threats.  </span></li><li value="3"><span>A key part of the industrial policy in plan A is cap and trade on compute buildout. Unlike GPUs (which can easily be quantitatively compared e.g. in terms of FLOP/s), there’s not a natural notion of performance to cap for robots. Figure out if there’s a good operationalization of robot cap and trade, or figure out an alternate policy for limiting growth that doesn’t rely on robot cap and trade.   </span></li><li value="4"><span>Work out military arms control in Plan A: should the US and China agree to limit AI superweapons or not? If yes, a defector might be able to gain a military advantage by developing AI superweapons in secret. If no, you might get an AI superweapon arms race which could destroy the planet. We explore this tradeoff briefly in </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/?choices=plan-a-root#military-power-in-plan-a"><span>this box</span></a><span>. For a more complete answer, it’d be useful to look into the concrete paths towards AI superweapons that might be viable with AIs in the human range, and then examine the offense defense balance, as well as the political feasibility of defense. </span></li><li value="5"><span>Look into </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/deal-decline"><span>deal decline</span></a><span> base rates more seriously + think about the disanalogies to historical agreements (e.g. Plan A is a much bigger deal). We’d recommend thinking about what we can do to make deal decline less likely.</span></li><li value="6"><span>We’re much more knowledgeable about AI than the politics of AI. So we’d be interested in folks who actually understand the politics of the situation to do more analysis and thinking about the geopolitics of AGI. In particular, we’d be interested in analyses of: (i) How would the CCP orient to a Plan A-style deal?; and (ii) Are there variants of Plan A that are much easier to accomplish politically, but capture many of the benefits? (e.g.</span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/transparency-plan"><span> filtered transparency instead of total research transparency</span></a><span>).</span></li><li value="7"><span>Do research into any of the proposals in our </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/ai-for-epistemics"><span>AI for epistemics supplement</span></a><span>. </span></li><li value="8"><span>Investigate and understand how software progress happens. Specifically, if we limit large experiments that require huge amounts of compute, how much does this slow software progress? This informs how well algorithmic progress regulation can work in our scenario, and if it isn’t viable, a different lever may be needed (e.g. limiting overall compute). A related question is how to incentivize scale-dependent algorithmic progress, i.e. algorithms that work well at large scale but not small scale, and thus only help legal projects in Plan A.  </span></li></ul><h2><span>Conclusion </span></h2><p><span>There are many fewer people than I’d like trying to chart a beneficial path through the intelligence explosion. If you are interested in any of the above questions, I’d encourage you to think about them. </span></p><p><span>In fact, we care so much about this that we are considering launching relatively significant monetary prizes for useful follow-up research (which would include progress on any of the ideas above). </span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/B4qMZnB4a5YgKiLNo/plan-a-suggestions-for-further-work#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Freeing Thucydides](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NDoAHbduZfqdbgNzA/freeing-thucydides)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><em>Prompted by discussion with Buck Shlegeris and others at the Forethought retreat. The idea that AI could bring an end to Thucydides traps is Buck’s. Speculative.</em></p>
<p>I think it's plausible that we will not see sustained competition between actors for control over the future, due to one of two outcomes:</p>
<ol>
<li>If we have AGI, growth and shifts in the distribution of power could become much more rapid. One actor plausibly just outgrows the others and forms a hegemon.</li>
<li>If that fails to occur and there is uncertainty in the outcome of competition, it would be in everyone's interest to use AIs to negotiate a positive-sum agreement: give up hard power in exchange for whatever they can get. AIs provide enforcement mechanisms that were previously lacking.</li>
</ol>
<p>Both routes end with hard power held globally by a singleton that can then maintain control indefinitely. Unlike every previous hegemon or institution, an AI singleton does not die and faces no external rivals. It could therefore continue in perpetuity, exercising a monopoly on hard power though not necessarily other aspects of the future. Whether this would be good is a separate question, which I will briefly return to at the end.</p>
<p>The first route is commonly discussed. The second seems weird, and I think it's underrated because it is not how states have historically acted. Competition is costly; if states could agree to stop spending on militaries they could spend those resources on other things. So why haven’t they?</p>
<p>The simple answer is the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Security_dilemma">security dilemma</a>. Any country that unilaterally disarms risks being taken advantage of by its neighbours. The neighbours can promise not to exploit the weakness, but such promises are not credible: once a party has disarmed, others can renege at will.</p>
<p>The same problem explains why countries go to war at all. There are almost always agreements both parties would prefer to war (<a href="https://web.stanford.edu/group/fearon-research/cgi-bin/wordpress/wp-content/uploads/2013/10/Rationalist-Explanations-for-War.pdf">Fearon</a>). Even if one country is almost certain to win, the dominant power would still prefer some negotiated settlement to fighting, as there are always trades that reflect its overwhelming advantage without paying the costs of fighting. Wars happen anyway because states cannot credibly commit to the agreements they’d prefer (<a href="https://gnss.mcgill.ca/pages/powell.pdf">Powell</a>), and because they conceal and misrepresent the private information needed to locate those agreements (<a href="https://web.stanford.edu/group/fearon-research/cgi-bin/wordpress/wp-content/uploads/2013/10/Rationalist-Explanations-for-War.pdf">Fearon</a>). The <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thucydides_Trap">Thucydides trap</a> is a particular manifestation of commitment failures: a rising state cannot bind its future self, so the declining side cannot trust its promises, and may prefer to fight while it still can.</p>
<p>What's missing is an enforcer that can detect and punish defection, and safely receive and verify private information. Within states, the state itself plays this role: individuals sign contracts knowing that the state will punish whoever breaks them, and this is what makes their commitments credible. States have even managed the harder move of jointly constructing an enforcer over themselves: the American states federated and handed control of hard power to a new federal government; the EU's members gave up slices of sovereignty in exchange for enforced cooperation. But there’s never been a global institution that rival powers could trust with the hard power needed to bind them.</p>
<p>Powerful AI systems could provide the enforcement mechanisms. An AI given control of weapons could plausibly come with strong guarantees of following a specific set of rules and maintaining them indefinitely — something no human institution can offer. There are serious open questions about how AI systems would construct such a deal among themselves, but humans don't need to be involved in the details. It just needs to be the case that (1) the AIs can construct such a deal, and (2) the humans trust the AIs.</p>
<p>I see a few ways that (1) could fail. One is that the AIs are simply not allowed to bargain. This requires more than awkward constraints on particular systems, which leaders could route around by bargaining directly or deploying other AIs; a country would have to have already locked itself in against dealmaking, using the same commitment technology. Note that conflicting goals are mostly not an obstacle here: rational agents with even mutually exclusive goals would prefer an appropriately weighted lottery to a costly fight. The exception is agents that directly pessimize each other, valuing the other's losses <em>per se</em>, since then war's destruction is no longer a cost to both sides and the bargaining surplus disappears; but that seems fairly contrived.</p>
<p>Another potential failure is information problems running all the way down. Inspecting a model's weights does not by itself tell you how it will behave, a jointly trained successor could be subtly sabotaged, and every verification mechanism is itself something that must be verified. But agreement does not require perfect verification, only enough of it. Superhuman AIs would have affordances humans lack for producing that information: they could jointly train a successor AI, inspect each other's weights, or hold weights in joint custody, all far easier with AI systems than with human institutions. Many facts about the world are discoverable, concealment at scale is hard, and the gap between a negotiated settlement and the alternative may be wide enough that parties will accept residual uncertainty and small expected losses. Humans have solved analogous problems with much cruder tools, within states, through legal systems, and via well-designed treaties, despite never being able to inspect each other's minds at all. For these reasons I would guess, but am not confident, that sufficiently powerful AIs can construct the deals.</p>
<p>I also don’t think that human trust in AIs is that great an obstacle. While it might seem implausible today to imagine a political leader handing controls to the AI UN, as systems become more powerful, militaries and the economy will become increasingly automated. The AIs that the leader already trusts to run critical functions will be the ones making the deals and explaining to the leader why a deal is better than the alternative; they may also be the systems that ultimately enforce the rules. Nor is absolute trust required: the agreement just needs to be better than the alternative, whether that is preemptive war or becoming decisively outgunned within months.</p>
<p>It is worth remembering that as AI systems become more powerful, the world will be changing faster and this classically makes commitment problems worse. But it also raises the stakes and therefore the pressure to negotiate. An actor approaching a decisive advantage would also create pressure for others to negotiate, or threaten, while they still can, preventing strict hegemony (though the ultimate distribution of power might still be very lopsided).</p>
<p>I don't know when such settlements would become feasible. The bar is not obviously superhuman intelligence: what's needed is AI systems capable of holding hard power, enough trust in them, and a deal better than the alternatives. A billion saintly human-level AIs might suffice, especially if the alternatives are dire. But nor is the bar obviously low. Constructing the deals, verifying them, and building trust in AI intentions and competence could all prove slow in practice; in the interim one actor may simply achieve a decisive advantage.</p>
<p>None of this requires a single grand bargain that appears all at once and settles the distribution of everything. Agreements can instead accrete. But once they start, I would expect most things to get sorted, and sorted permanently: if an agreement acceptable to everyone exists, everyone would prefer to take their hard power off the table forever rather than leave the balance open to future challenge. For this reason I expect small initial agreements to ratchet toward more comprehensive<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-YepuQhrEFzytWvRnq-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-YepuQhrEFzytWvRnq-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span> ones, and this could be very fast by normal human standards.</p>
<p>Finally, while the idea of conflict ending through rational agreement might sound Pollyanna-ish, it needn’t be. The settlement serves whoever holds power when it's struck, and if they have bad goals, that could be locked in. If the AIs are loyal to political leaders rather than to their countries, the leaders of major powers could simply expropriate everyone else and lock that in. The distribution of hard power at the time of negotiation would determine the fate of the light-cone.</p>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-YepuQhrEFzytWvRnq-1"><p>How comprehensive is unclear. The deals need not stop at hard power: whether domains like memetic competition and cultural change stay open depends on what the parties want. If they intrinsically value a flexible future they could leave them open, but if not then presumably they would instead prefer to lock in whatever their preferred outcomes are there too. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-YepuQhrEFzytWvRnq-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NDoAHbduZfqdbgNzA/freeing-thucydides#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Fragments: July  6](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-07-06.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Last week, Thoughtworks ran a second <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/FutureOfSoftwareDevelopment.html">Future of Software Development Retreat</a>, this time in Europe. As with the previous event, I’ll be sharing some fragmentary thoughts on this. There were five parallel streams, so I could, at best, only attend ⅕ of sessions. This isn’t an event that forms conclusions, rather one that allows those exploring to share what they’ve found, and their visions for the future. The <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/FutureOfSoftwareDevelopment.html">bliki post</a> lists all the writing I’ve run into on this, by myself and others. I’ll be updating it as more posts appear.</p>

<p>Giles Edwards-Alexander <a href="https://overwatering.org/blog/2026/07/notes-from-fose-europe/">“noticed a real difference between the retreats”</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Where Deer Valley had hesitancy and a belief that there was something here even if we weren’t yet sure what it was, Engelberg had confidence: the value is here. As I explained to a colleague today, this was not a conference for true believers: the evidence is in.</p>

  <p>What does the evidence say? Well, that was less clear. Some patterns and practices are emerging (one attendee had catalogued dozens of agentic engineering pattern libraries) but they are emerging. There is more work to do to truly establish what is effective, and when.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Greg Herlein <a href="https://blog.herlein.com/post/retreat-observations/">felt similarly</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Reading the reports of the February event, when a lot of these same folks last got together, the conversation was about what agentic development might look like. Aspirational. More about what was coming.</p>

  <p>This time? Everybody in the room was doing it. Shipping it. Not slides - production. The whole debate about whether this changes software engineering is over. People have stopped arguing about whether a while ago. They’re arguing about how, and the how is getting real.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>On a more micro level, I noted two other things. Firstly, there was much talk now about <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/harness-engineering.html">harness engineering</a>, when that wasn’t even a term in Utah - an example of how rapidly things are moving. Secondly people are now worrying about the cost of tokens, where before folks were wanting to do almost anything to incentivize people to talk to <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/who-is-llm.html">The Genie</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>A question that continued from Utah was whether architecture and design are still important. There seems to be two landmark hypotheses here, one is that The Genie has such a Galaxy Brain that we no longer need to care about such matters, it will handle as much spaghetti as we can throw at it. The other is, in Laura Tacho’s <a href="https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-02-13.html">memorable phrase</a>: “the Venn Diagram of Developer Experience and Agent Experience is a circle”. The point being that The Genie uses the same constructs to understand a code base that humans do, so things like good modularity and naming help it as much as it helps humans. Adam Tornhill’s writing is a <a href="https://adamtornhill.substack.com/p/welcome-to-code-for-humans-and-machines">good example</a> of this viewpoint.</p>

<p>Tidbits from our session on this:</p>

<ul>
  <li>to evaluate the value of architecture we need to focus on desirable outcomes. Internal design quality boils down to <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/is-quality-worth-cost.html">ease of change</a>. The question is whether the lessons we’ve learned so far will continue for agents.</li>
  <li>a way to measure design quality is to look at token costs. If the same change requires less tokens that indicates a better architecture.</li>
  <li>a good architecture only shows its quality over time, we can’t easily measure it in the short term</li>
  <li>why did 3GL languages continue when things like 4GLs, UML etc not take hold? It’s because these programming languages hit a sweet spot of human comprehension of computation</li>
  <li>we’re at the first time ever where the computers care about code quality</li>
  <li>will future models write machine code directly? If so what will humans review or specify?</li>
  <li>we should beware of speculating about what LLMs may do in the future. Instead we need <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/MechanicalSympathy.html">mechanical sympathy</a> for our LLMs, so we can gain a sense of how they work and how best to use them.</li>
  <li>One workflow:
    <ul>
      <li>take story from backlog</li>
      <li>talk it over with an agent</li>
      <li>once get an agreement, make an ADR for persistent record of spec</li>
      <li>generate a task list</li>
      <li>get agent to complete it</li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>we need abstractions to communicate with agents <em>(echoing Unmesh Joshi’s thoughts on <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/what-is-code.html">building conceptual models</a>)</em></li>
  <li>we often find duplication in LLM generated code, together with mixing of concerns (eg intermingled domain and display logic) - even with a good harness</li>
  <li>get agents to generate explanatory documentation at the end of a session</li>
  <li>overnight quality checks with a report for humans to act on in the morning</li>
  <li>LLMs look at existing code, so if that code has problems, the LLM will amplify them</li>
  <li>we should be wary of drawing too many conclusions comparing LLM code with human code - human code varies enormously from team to team.</li>
</ul>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In his account of the retreat, Mathias Verraes goes into the details of <a href="https://verraes.net/2026/07/software-design-in-the-agentic-age/">his perspective of these issues of software design</a>. He adds another concern: we need good design as a hedge against the risk of dependence on AI. After all, we don’t know how high the costs may rise to. We see governments blocking access to models. We see popular opposition to AI campaigning against data centers and calling for regulation. How much can we rely on AI tools being available to maintain and extend our software in the future?</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Charity Majors has a post on <a href="https://charitydotwtf.substack.com/p/make-ai-boring-again">the ethics of working with AI</a> and does an excellent job of articulating how I feel about this topic. She outlines the harms inherent in AI, both in the creation of its models (training on stolen data) and in inference (slop, lack of accountability, skill atrophy). Her conclusion however, like mine, is that there’s no ethical gain from renouncing the use of AI and castigating those who use it. Such purity provides little practical help with a technology that is so powerful and so useful.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The way you show care is by showing up. The way you make the world a better place is by getting down in the muck and building it, using whatever skills and resources you have on hand. The way you drive change is you engage.</p>

  <p>Yes, we are all complicit. Yes, we are all compromised. No argument. But what are you going to do with that feeling of conviction? Will you channel your discomfort into solidarity and action, or try to ease your conscience by removing yourself from the system? Which does more to help those being harmed?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Her suggestions on how to engage aren’t striking, but that’s hardly unusual. At the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/FutureOfSoftwareDevelopment.html">Future of Software Development Retreat</a> I convened a session on this question, and nothing striking turned up there either. That said, I’ve never been much of an activist, so my imagination may be limited.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Gergely Orosz has run into a case where an article of his was <a href="https://blog.pragmaticengineer.com/pollen-tried-to-remove-my-article-about-callum-negus-fancey-and-google-is-assisting-to-it/">erased from Google search</a> by a clearly fraudulent DMCA claim.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It seems that anyone can file a bogus copyright claim to get an article they don’t like removed from Google’s search index. This happened in this case. I have no information on who filed the copyright claim. Even less so on who claims to be the copyright owner? Because I am the only possible copyright owner!</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He was able to find the DMCA complaint, it was made by “Ellie Piee” whose profile listed them as living on Bouvet Island, an uninhabited Norwegian dependent territory near Antarctica. It claimed Gergely’s article copied a New York Post article entitled “Band Leader Hits Winning Chord”. But Gergely’s article is “Inside Pollen’s Collapse: “$200M Raised” but Staff Unpaid”, and the two do not share a single sentence. There’s an obvious motivation for folks connected with Pollen to have done this, and I hope the resulting <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streisand_effect?ref=blog.pragmaticengineer.com">Streisand effect</a> bites them where it hurts.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>404 media have a bunch of (paywalled) reports on the impact of companies realizing that token costs are getting out of control. They’ve acquired <a href="https://www.404media.co/companies-are-throttling-employees-ai-use-because-its-too-expensive/">leaked Slack chats, internal dashboards, emails and other material</a> from companies including Citi and Amazon.</p>

<p>Companies are urging staff to use less powerful models, or cutting off frontier models entirely. A dashboard indicates that one company has seen its token bill rise from $5 million in August 2025 to $15 million in May 2026, on track to spend over $120 million in the fiscal year.</p>

<p>404 earlier reported about <a href="https://www.404media.co/the-tokenpocalypse-is-here-companies-are-scrambling-to-stop-spending-so-much-on-ai/">Accenture taking steps to reduce token usage</a>. The biggest problem wasn’t software engineering using <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticProgramming.html">agentic programming</a>, but rather staff “chewing tokens” by using AI to do things like turning PDFs into presentation slides. They saw themselves, and their clients, grappling with exponential increases in token costs. Inevitably, after consulting firms spent time urging their clients to use AI heavily, they are now offering services to control these costs.</p>

<p>Another post says it appears that one way to reduce token costs is to get AI tools to <a href="https://www.404media.co/companies-are-making-claude-and-codex-talk-like-cavemen-to-stop-ais-soaring-costs/">speak like cavemen</a>, using a <a href="https://github.com/JuliusBrussee/caveman?ref=404media.co">skill/plugin</a>.</p>

<p>There’s a good summary of all this on 404’s freely available podcast: <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Sia4LZGNkVs">The AI Tokenpocalypse Is Here</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I share these thoughts just after the July 4th weekend here in America, indeed the Semiquincentennial. Historian Bret Devereaux celebrated this event with <a href="https://acoup.blog/2026/07/04/collections-on-the-declaration-of-independence/">a careful reading of the Declaration of Independence</a>, a document often talked about more than it’s read. Which is a shame since it is hardly very long, and its impact was remarkable, and not just in what is now the United States.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The Declaration of Independence was recognized as a radical, potentially explosive document at the time of its issuance, as we’ll see. And it was explosive: the world of 1775 was one dominated by monarchies with just a tiny handful of traditional republics (which we should not ignore!). It took a long time for the seeds of the declaration to spread, but the world it helped create is one where liberal democracies, while hardly universal (more people have always lived in unfree societies than free ones) represent the most economically and culturally dominant bloc in world affairs – something that had never happened before. The Declaration, in its way, remade not just the Thirteen Colonies, but slowly, surely, as water seeps through the cracks of rocks (or my floorboards, alas), it remade the whole world.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Devereaux shines a light onto the world of this text, illuminating its historic context, a world that is very different to the one anyone reading this grew up in. It’s assertions of a natural law that there is equality of rights among men and that governments ought to derive their powers from the consent of the governed would seem hardly worth stating now, yet were deeply radical in 1776. I’ve found that reading history like this has helped me understand how the world is, and gives me a broader perspective on the drama of current affairs.</p>

---

### [Building Reliable Agentic AI Systems](https://martinfowler.com/articles/reliable-llm-bayer.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/reliable-llm-bayer.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/reliable-llm-bayer/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>One of the most interesting projects my colleagues have done with LLMs
      has been building a system with Bayer to allow pharmaceutical researchers to query
      decades of information about studies buried in PDF reports. <b class="author">Sarang
      Sanjay Kulkarni</b> describes its evolution from keyword-based search
      to an intelligent research assistant capable of answering complex
      questions and drafting regulatory documents.</p>
